@@131962727 <p> Deborah Dundas is an editor with the Star 's opinion section . She is based in Toronto . Follow her on Twitter : @debdundas . <p> How do we know who we are ? And what messages might we want to send those who come after us -- and will they be likely to listen ? In his latest book , " What We Can Know , " celebrated UK writer Ian McEwan explores these questions by wrapping them in a mystery : a poem by fictional writer Francis Blundy is read aloud at a dinner party in 2014 and then disappears . One hundred years later , much of the Western world has been submerged by floods , and a researcher becomes obsessed with finding the poem . His foray into archives leaves him longing for the possibilities and promise of the early twenty-first century . Deborah Dundas spoke with McEwan by phone -- beginning by talking about the idea that expertise is no longer valued in the world . <p> I 'm wondering if that might be a place to start , because there 's a contempt @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ elements of the populist right , this is true . Meanwhile , the people who are saying that are getting on airplanes and stepping into elevators and generally putting their lives in the hands of experts , mechanics and engineers and pilots . So I think it 's bullshit . It 's what they say when they do n't want to believe inconvenient truth . <p> ARTICLE CONTINUES BELOW <p> ARTICLE CONTINUES BELOW <p> I mean , you know , the same MAGA crowd who distrust experts generally are living by conspiracy theories and anti science . It 's widespread and it 's worrying . <p> Fair . We 're also seeing it , though , in the lack of critical thinking about books , for example , or a distrust of the media . What are we to make of that -- that it 's not just MAGA supporters ? <p> No , that 's true . I mean , I think the internet has obviously made inroads into people 's ability to think critically . At the same time , if you follow closely news developments in the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ age was , I reckon that human ability to en masse pay attention to detail over extended periods of time has always been pretty limited . I 'm very cautious about the general sense of decline . <p> I hear often that people say kids are spending all their time staring at screens and they 've lost the ability to socialize . Well , you know , kids used to spend all their time reading books , wasting their time . No one was socializing when they were reading " War and Peace . " I think the attention span is a biological matter . In other words , I think you could quickly get people to pay lengthy attention to things if they have to . When was this golden age , when people had massive attention ? I do n't know . <p> So after the " great inundation " you speak about in your book -- where the world 's been flooded and some cities have disappeared -- there 's still a desire for understanding . There 's an argument in there for the importance of the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , but speculating , I also think about what moral responsibilities we in the present have to the unborn people of the future . For example , it took thousands of years to develop a free crop rotation . It took a long time to learn how to make glass out of sand . So foundations like the Long Now Foundation have been concentrating on sending information into the future as part of a moral quest to exercise responsibility . So that 's the background to writing this . <p> ARTICLE CONTINUES BELOW <p> ARTICLE CONTINUES BELOW <p> We can become overwhelmed by information . Within the novel , the characters are doing research into the author of the poem and are having to wade through every email they 'd ever sent , including all of the banality . <p> Exactly , you 'll know a lot more about everyday life , I suppose . Perhaps less about the kinds of things we 'd find in 19th century : 3,000-word letters by Dickens or Darwin or Napoleon . So there are losses and gains . <p> You 've written in @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ creativity under pressure , or science being a creative endeavour . <p> I was drawing parallels between the search for originality in science and literature . <p> But I just want to come back Deborah , to finish this thought on climate change . There is a metaphysical aspect to climate change , when people start losing faith in the future . I think some colossal , near-invisible shift starts to happen in human consciousness , because they also lose hope in the idea of progress . When that happens I think that chaos can ensue . I mean the kinds of things we associate with social breakdown and warfare . To lose hope in the notion of human progress is a kind of poison if you do n't think you can make life better for those who are in other countries , who are fighting for a decent life , or within your own society , for people who do n't have proper access to not only to food security or home security , but also to all the delights and privileges of access to knowledge and education . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ world governed by billionaires who are cynical about any of that , then I think you cross the line into kind of social despair . <p> When we think what we are going to leave our grandchildren , one idea is that they might look back on the bits of and pieces of what they can get to know of the present day , of the past , and be haunted " by the richness of the world that has been lost . " <p> ARTICLE CONTINUES BELOW <p> ARTICLE CONTINUES BELOW <p> If things go badly , that 's almost certainly going to be the case , and they 'll be completely baffled that we did continue to do the things we did when we knew full well what a bad idea it was to be taking down our primary forests , to be extinguishing species on an extraordinary industrial scale and , above all , to be burning fossil fuels when we had perfectly good ways of propelling things with electricity -- especially that electricity can be generated from trillions and trillions of photons that bombard the Earth @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , and we will be considered the bad old days , and thank God we did n't live . But I think that the point is that we 're likely to have some pretty major disasters this century . Will we or wo n't we get to the end of the century without an exchange of nuclear weapons ? Will we get through holding temperatures , let 's say , to two degrees ? Even that is going to be very , very disruptive , but we would scrape through . We can read our history books and see acts of unbelievable barbarity , but they have n't gone away -- we witness the scale of suffering in Gaza or in South Sudan or every night in Ukraine . It seems as if we are maybe desensitizing ourselves to brutality . <p> How do we not cross that line ? When you wrote of Einstein and Darwin and their feats of creativity -- is a feat of creativity on that scale still possible ? <p> Oh , I think so . I always take an interest in artificial intelligence . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ leaps forward in that and it 's very frightening , as well as very interesting . And the amount of ingenuity that 's being poured into it is extraordinary . We do n't know what we 're going to do with it or what it 's going to do to us , but the ingenuity ca n't be doubted . I mean , the thing is , creativity , ingenuity can be forced for good or bad , it 's morally neutral , I think , as a term . So I do n't doubt human cleverness . But ... there 's a way of being very clever that generates colossal stupidity . Anyone who 's worked in a university knows just how stupid clever people can be . Indeed , you 'll know that working for a newspaper , The Star , incredibly clever people in the building can be unbelievably stupid . So we 're almost like a juggler with just two balls in the air , one ball called clever , the other called stupid . <p> There we are . So , are you hopeful ? @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ that the alternative is inaction and despair and becoming moribund . So yes , I retain a very important , strong thread of optimism , but it has to be a nuanced optimism . I know we 're going to go through some bad stuff , sure , but I think we 'll scrape through . I do n't think we 're going to burst through into some sunny upland easily . And look how we scraped through the 20th century , two major world wars -- in the second one , maybe as many as 90 million dead -- a city like Tokyo , for the third time , razed to the ground and then get busy again . <p> Think of those pictures we have of Europe in 1945 . Who would imagine 30 years later , bustling , fairly successful places ? I rather think the future is going to be something like the past in that respect . We 'll scrape through , we 'll build up , then we might smash it down , ( and ) we 'll build it up again . <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ roundup of the best from our columnists . <p> Error ! @@131963501 <h> Stories ThatInspireYou <p> As the year ends , our reporting does not slow down . Support the journalists who continue asking questions and telling stories with depth and care . Your membership today helps ensure we start the new year strong . <h> This Rising Baby Name Highlights What Our Society Needs <p> Not long ago , Truce was almost unheard of as a given name . Now , it 's the fastest-rising boys ' name in the U.S. <p> According to the Social Security Administration 's latest data , Truce rose a whopping 11,118 places in the ranking from the 12,109th most popular name for boys in 2023 to No. 991 in 2024 . <p> " The rise of the baby name Truce has been stunning , " Abby Sandel , the creator of the baby name blog Appellation Mountain , told HuffPost . " In raw numbers , six boys were named Truce in 2023 . By 2024 , there were 227 newborn boys called Truce , as well as 10 girls . " <p> Advertisement <p> She added that it 's a " true overnight sensation @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ ? la the influence of Miley Cyrus on the name Miley , the show " Yellowstone " for Dutton , or the book and movie " The Hate U Give " for the name Sekani . <p> " There have been a handful of high-profile men named Truce , " Sandel said . " Grant Stuard made his first NFL touchdown in 2023 ? his dad is named Truce , and their family 's story generated some media coverage . There 's a rapper , as well as a Twenty One Pilots song . " <p> None of those links feel strong or definitive enough for her , however . <p> " Truce is one of the rare risers that comes out of thin air without a media catalyst ? at least , none that we could find , " echoed Sophie Kihm , the editor-in-chief of Nameberry . " But Truce fits into a couple of major trends that are influencing its rise . " <p> Advertisement <p> She believes the name is a good example of what she describes as " soulful names " ? modern , spiritual @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ category Nameberry identified for 2026 . <p> " These include meaningful word names like Shepherd and Sage , Vision and Verse , Tru and , of course , Truce , " Kihm wrote . " Truce may not seem like a religious word name on the surface ? few of our Soulful Names do ? but parents are drawn to these choices because they blend spirituality with modern style . " <h> A Response To Parents ' ' Fatigue ' <p> Another factor Kihm sees playing into the rise of Truce is the news coverage of major wars in our world , particularly the Russia-Ukraine war and Israel-Gaza conflict . <p> Advertisement <p> " Historically , wars have had large influences on baby names . German-origin names largely fell in usage after WWI , for example , " she said . " In recent years , we 've seen an uptick in what we call ' safe haven ' names that promote peace and refuge ? think Haven , Eden , Harbor . " <p> She sees a name like Truce as an even more overt reference to safety and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of the name Truce is a direct result of the fatigue parents feel from all the wars and violence in the world right now , " Kihm noted . <p> Advertisement <p> Vera Livchak via Getty Images <p> The rise of Truce took many baby naming experts by surprise , but on closer examination , they say it makes sense . <p> Baby name consultant Taylor Humphrey also sees the rise of Truce as deeply tied to the cultural moment . <p> " I was surprised to learn that Truce was the fastest rising boy 's name last year , " she said . " It had n't crossed my radar before , but now that I see it , I totally understand its appeal . Its meteoric rise to prominence during a deeply divisive election year says a lot about the cultural temperature of the moment . In one syllable , Truce conveys a deep desire for calm amid global conflict . " <p> Advertisement <h> The name Truce is also appealing in its combination of trending sounds and positive meaning . <p> " It 's a brief , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ especially True , " Sandel said . " But it also fits with our love of modern virtue names like Chosen , Creed , and Saint . Plus Truce shares some of the swagger of neo-Western names like Stetson , Remington , and Boone . And , of course , Truce is the Gen Beta descendant of Boomer favorite Bruce . It 's a formula for a wildly successful name . " <p> The ' tru ' -sound and ' oo ' -sounds are part of a broader sonic pattern dominating contemporary baby names . <p> " In addition to Truce , Tru and Truett were among the list of fast rising baby names between 2023 and 2024 . True , Truth , and Truly also rose modestly in use , " Kihm said . " Truce also shares sounds with fashionable names like Lucy , Lucian , Luca , Jude , Luna , and Cruz . " <p> Advertisement <p> Humphrey described Truce as a nice mix between True and Bruce , bringing something old and familiar but also modern . <p> " This masculine moniker meaning ' peace @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the broader category of ' virtue names , ' " she added . " Virtue names are nothing new ? parents have always loved names that reflect their values ? but the specific virtues we choose evolve with the times , as cultural beliefs and attitudes ebb and flow . " <p> She pointed to the many millennial and Gen Z babies given names like Faith , Hope and Grace . <p> " Now , millennial and Gen Z parents are reaching for new names like Truce , modern virtue names born from a hope for world peace , " Humphrey said . " Whether or not a name is overtly ' virtuous , ' every name is a statement of values . Parents choosing Truce are signaling a longing for peace ? for a world where rules are clear , agreements are honored , and people feel safe . " <p> Advertisement <p> She recommends Truce to parents seeking a meaningful name that is immediately familiar but not too common in everyday speech . <p> " Word names often suggest a specific image , and there 's no guarantee @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . " Maverick might be shy , Patience could lose her temper . Grace might trip over her own feet . But Truce fits into a broadly aspirational word name category , one that suggests peace and strength . " <p> The name Truce could fit nicely with Haven or Harmony or work as a less-expected alternative to Sage , Sandel noted . <p> The rise of Truce might also signal a broader shift in baby-naming that 's exciting to experts . <p> " For generations , parents took more risks when naming daughters , but stuck with traditional choices and family heritage picks for our sons , " Sandel said . " It 's why classic boys ' names like Joseph and James are still in such heavy use . But that 's really changed in the 21st century . Parents are looking for boys ' names with just as much style and creativity . That 's opened the door for the rapid rise of Truce and other unexpected names , and that 's a positive and welcome change . "

@@131984203 <p> On his first foreign papal trip , Pope Leo XIV showed reporters that the inflight news conferences of his predecessors would continue . <p> And , in fact , he added the unusual twist Nov. 30 of answering reporters ' questions in the middle of a trip as he flew from Turkey to Lebanon . <p> During the two-hour flight , he came to the journalists ' section of the plane , spoke about the ecumenical importance of his trip and answered questions posed by two Turkish journalists . <p> Advertisement <p> He had met privately with Turkish President Recep Tayyip Erdogan Nov. 27 , the first day of his trip , but neither the Vatican nor the president had issued a statement about their conversation . <p> A reporter asked if they had discussed the ongoing wars in Ukraine and Gaza ; the pope said they had . <p> " On this trip , there was a special theme of being a messenger of peace , of wanting to promote peace throughout the region , " Pope Leo said . <p> As for Gaza , he said , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ a proposal for a two-state solution " in the Holy Land with Israel and Palestine being fully independent nations , secure within defined borders . <p> " We all know that right now Israel does not accept that solution , but we see it as the only possible solution to the conflict they continually live , " Pope Leo said . <p> Advertisement <p> " We are also friends with Israel , " he said , " and with both sides we try to be a mediating voice that can help approach a solution with justice for all . @@131984866 <p> Pope Leo XIV landed in Lebanon Sunday , where he aimed to bring a message of hope to its long-suffering people and bolster a crucial Christian community in the Middle East . <p> He arrived in Lebanon at a precarious moment for the small Mediterranean country after years of successive crises . He is fulfilling a promise of Pope Francis , who had wanted to visit for years , but was unable to as his health worsened . <p> Lebanon is the second leg of Leo 's first official foreign trip after he first visited Turkey to mark an important Christian anniversary at a time where the predominantly Sunni Muslim country of over 85 million has positioned itself as a key intermediary in talks to end the conflicts in Ukraine and the Gaza Strip . <p> Leo had two key appointments in Istanbul before flying to Beirut : a prayer at the Armenian Apostolic Cathedral and a divine liturgy with Ecumenical Patriarch Bartholomew , spiritual leader of the world 's Orthodox Christians , whose invitation was the impetus for his visit . <p> Leo processed into the Armenian cathedral @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . He praised the " courageous Christian witness of the Armenian people throughout history , often amid tragic circumstances . " It was a reference to the World War I-era slaughter of Armenians by Ottoman Turks . <p> Pope Francis had termed the massacre a " genocide , " angering Turkey , which denies a genocide took place . Leo was more diplomatic in his words on Turkish soil . <p> In Lebanon , Leo was expected to try to encourage Lebanese who believe their leaders have failed them , and to call on Lebanese Christians to stay or , if they have already moved abroad , to come home . <p> Francis often quoted St. John Paul II , who in 1989 said that Lebanon was more than just a country . It was a " message " -- a message of fraternity and coexistence . Under Lebanon 's power-sharing system , the country 's president is always a Maronite Christian , the prime minister a Sunni Muslim and the parliamentary speaker a Shiite . <p> Lebanon , a Muslim-majority country where about a third of the population is @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , a bulwark for Christians throughout the region . After years of conflict , Christian communities that date from the time of the Apostles have shrunk . <p> " The Holy Father is coming at a very difficult moment for Lebanon and for our region , " said Bishop George , archbishop of the Melkite Greek Catholic Archeparchy of Beirut . Lebanese are worried about the future , he said , and still fear a possible return to all-out war with Israel . <p> " In this difficult moment , the pope 's visit is a sign of hope . It shows that Lebanon is not forgotten , " he told reporters before the visit . <h> Series of crises <p> In 2019 , the country 's currency and banking system collapsed and many Lebanese saw their savings evaporate . The financial crisis drove shortages of electricity , fuel and medicine . <p> Another disaster followed in 2020 , when hundreds of tons of ammonium nitrate improperly stored at the Beirut port detonated in an explosion that blasted through the surrounding neighborhoods , killing 218 people , wounding thousands more @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ highlight of Leo 's Lebanese visit will come on his last day , Dec. 2 , when he spends time in silent prayer at the site of the blast on Aug. 4 , 2020 , and meets with some of its victims . <p> Lebanese citizens were enraged by the blast , which appeared to be the result of government negligence , coming on top of the economic crisis . But an investigation has repeatedly stalled , and five years on , no official has been convicted . <p> There are hopes among Lebanese that Leo will demand accountability from Lebanon 's political class and insist that there can be no peace without truth and justice . <p> Another important moment will come when Leo meets with young Lebanese . He 's expected to give them words of encouragement , amid the decades-long flight abroad , while also acknowledging their disillusionment over the failures of generations before them . <h> Lebanon 's ongoing conflict with Israel <p> After the Hamas-led attack on southern Israel on Oct. 7 , 2023 , which sparked the war in Gaza , the Lebanese militant @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ escalated into a full-fledged war in September 2024 , killing more than 4,000 people in Lebanon and causing widespread destruction . <p> Despite a U.S.-brokered ceasefire that nominally ended the conflict two months later , Israel continues to launch near-daily airstrikes that it says aim to stop Hezbollah from rebuilding . <p> The pope " is coming to bless us and for the sake of peace , " said Farah Saadeh , a Beirut resident walking on the city 's seaside promenade . " We have to wait and see what will happen after he leaves , and we hope nothing is going to happen after his departure . " <p> Before Leo 's arrival , Hezbollah urged the pope to express his " rejection to injustice and aggression " that the country is being subjected to . It was a reference to the Israeli strikes . The group also urged its supporters to line up along the road the papal convoy will take from the airport to the presidential palace to pay their respects . <p> Hezbollah -- a primarily Shiite group -- has allied with several Christian political @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and Marada Movement . <p> However , the Christian party with the largest parliamentary bloc , the Lebanese Forces , is an opponent of Hezbollah and has criticized the group for pulling the country into a war with Israel . <h> Syrian Christians <p> In neighboring Syria , hundreds of thousands of Christians fled during the country 's 14 years of civil war . <p> The country 's former autocratic President Bashar Assad was ousted in an offensive led by Islamist insurgents last December . Since then , there have been outbreaks of sectarian violence and some attacks on religious minorities , including a suicide attack on a church in Damascus in June . <p> While the new government has condemned attacks on minorities , many accuse it of looking the other way or being unable to control allied armed groups . <p> A delegation of some 300 Syrian Christians , headed by a Greek Melkite Catholic priest , was set to travel to Lebanon to join a meeting between Leo and youth groups and pray in a public mass on Beirut 's waterfront . <p> " We are in @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ us hope as Christians " at a time of " fear of an unknown future , " said 24-year-old Dima Awwad , one of the delegation members . " We wish that the pope would come to visit Syria as he visited Lebanon , to reassure the people and to feel that we are present as eastern Christians and that we need to be in this place . ( AP Photo/Ghaith Alsayed , File ) <h> The top 100 photos of 2025 from The Associated Press : Images that defined the year <p> Years come and go , sometimes before we even realize that time has passed . Events blur and run together . The news is overwhelming , and even those who follow it closely can feel a sense of unremitting vertigo . Such is 21st-century life on a connected and chaotic planet . <p> Years come and go , sometimes before we even realize that time has passed . Events blur and run together . The news is overwhelming , and even those who follow it closely can feel a sense of unremitting vertigo . Such is 21st-century life on a connected and chaotic planet . <p> <p> EDITOR 'S NOTE -- This gallery contains some graphic images . <p> <p> But people -- people stand out , no matter where they may be . And moments in time have the power to freeze @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ shutter , over and over around the planet -- from Gaza to Ukraine , the Philippines to Haiti , Maine to the White House to California and so many points in between . <p> That 's what The Associated Press ' corps of photographers documented in 2025 : the frenzied and the quiet , the bloody and the contemplative , and even healthy doses of joy , wonder and discovery to help us see that our violent and sometimes inexplicable world is full of good things , too . <p> ARTICLE CONTINUES BELOW <p> In over 200 locations globally , photojournalists with the AP are trusted eyewitnesses to news and have won 36 of AP 's Pulitzer Prizes since the award was established in 1917 . <p> They are far more than eyewitnesses , though . They are journalists , explorers , artists , understanders . They are experts in vantage point and light , in people skills and storytelling . <p> Sometimes it 's the color : the saturated , angry oranges in Etienne Laurent 's arresting image of firefighters in California battling a blaze that had engulfed a @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 's the action and the movement : Robert F. Bukaty 's photo of the back half of a sturgeon , propelling itself fiercely through the waters of Maine and leaving bubbles in its wake . Or the kinetic energy that bursts from Aaron Favila 's frame of men massing to salvage electrical wire after a fire in a poor Philippine community . <p> Sometimes it 's what photographers call " negative space " : Petros Giannakouris ' image of the Parthenon and the moon at night , their magnificence dramatically amplified by the midnight-blue sky that fills most of the frame . <p> Sometimes it is juxtaposition across images and continents : Niranjan Shrestha 's photo of a demonstrator in Nepal , his hair cascading behind him and his arms outstretched after he grabbed a flak jacket and a shield from a policeman during the chaos of a protest -- and its mirror image of joy , Andy Wong 's image of a woman with her own arms in the air as she jumps into a pool carved from ice in frozen northeastern China . <p> ARTICLE CONTINUES BELOW <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ heartbreak , as in Julia Demaree Nikhinson 's close-up of Erika Kirk wiping a tear from her reddened right eye before speaking at a memorial for her husband , slain conservative activist Charlie Kirk . Or even double heartbreak : a Palestinian woman , gravely injured , holding the body of her infant daughter at Nasser Hospital in the Gaza Strip after an Israeli airstrike -- and the realization that mere weeks later , the photographer herself , Mariam Dagga , would die in another airstrike while covering the same hospital . <p> Finally , sometimes it is simply quiet contemplation -- a rarer moment in these jumbled days -- as seen through the lens of Jenny Kane , who captured the silhouette of a lone man walking on the beach near a rock jutting from shore at the edge of the land in Oregon . <p> Whatever the subject , whatever the memorable trait , one thing is constant . In every single image , AP photographers carefully calibrated their equipment -- both creative and mechanical -- to bring the world glimpses of itself that resist forgetting . <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , when only the images remain for most of us , that photographic act -- carving grooves of collective memory with color and light and verve and creativity -- may be one of the most important contributions of all . <p> <p> Photo editing by Benjamin Snyder , Enric Mart ? , and Jacqueline Larma . <p> ONTARIO NOW NEWSLETTER <p> Get our free new weekly newsletter <p> Error ! @@131994791 <h> Four Israeli leaders are coming to Canada to debate the two-state solution . Meet the ' no ' side <p> Former Israeli Ambassador to the U.S.A , Michael Oren , will be in Toronto this week to debate against a two-state solution for Israel and Palestinians . ( Submitted photo ) <p> Former Israeli Ambassador Michael Oren , a diplomat , historian , and former member of the Israeli Knesset , says he has always refused to do public " gladitorial " debates when it comes to representing Israel these last two decades in public life . But the American-born statesman and author changed his longstanding practice to come to Canada this Wednesday , Dec. 3 , to headline the Munk Debates onstage in Toronto . Organizers are mounting what they admit is their thorniest topic ever : be it resolved that supporting the two-state solution is in Israel 's best interest . <p> Oren is on the " no " side , together with former right-wing Israeli politician Ayelet Shaked . They 'll take on a former Israeli prime minister , Ehud Olmert , and former cabinet minister @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ yes " side . <p> The debate is already attracting controversy for several reasons . There were no Palestinian voices invited on the program , and organizers are expecting protests , so security has been ramped up . They also had to move from their traditional venue , Roy Thomson Hall , for the first time in 15 years . But despite the sideshow , Oren believes the Munk Debates are important to reach a massive online audience with reasoned arguments , including why most Israelis oppose the two-state solution in any near future . He calls the proposal " deranged " , especially after Oct. 7 , even though most Western countries , including Canada , are doubling down on the idea . <p> So what 's in store for Israel , Palestinians , and the Middle East ? Oren joins The CJN 's North Star podcast host Ellin Bessner on today 's episode to share his take . <p> From clockwise from top left : the debaters include Ehud Olmert , former Israeli prime minister , and Tzipi Livni , former peace negotiator and cabinet minister , for @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ former right of centre cabinet minister , and former ambassador Michael Oren . The policing costs will be offset by city revenue , according to the proposed budget . <p> The budget request notes that , according to statistics kept by Toronto police , homicides , shootings , hate crimes , robberies and auto thefts have all decreased compared to last year , as of Oct. 31 . There @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ fatalities . <p> The report attributes this in part to policing efforts . <p> There were fewer priority calls requiring a police response this year , a 12 per cent decrease from 2024 , and priority response times improved , the report notes . The highest priority police response time was an average of 13.5 minutes this year , a 23 per cent decrease from 2024 . <p> In August , police launched a non-emergency line shortcut on cellphones ( *877 ) to help stop people from calling 911 for non-urgent reasons . The city is also hiring more 911 operators . <p> Carroll said the statistics are " wonderful " but " we do n't want to take our hand off the steering wheel at this point . @@132036880 <p> Good morning . Canada 's beloved Franklin the Turtle has zipped his way through countless dilemmas , but now he 's at the centre of a controversy involving America 's war on drugs -- more on that below , along with weddings in Gaza and rescue efforts in Asia . But first : <h> U.S. Politics <h> Shell shocked <p> Franklin the Turtle can do a great deal . He can count forward and backward . He can zip zippers and button buttons . He can sell 65 million books in more than 30 languages , star in a TV show with a catchy Bruce Cockburn theme song , appear on postage stamps and a thoroughly ' 90s Maclean'smagazine cover , and teach generations of preschoolers how to problem-solve their way out of a jam . <p> But Franklin now finds himself in an especially dicey situation : the crosshairs of America 's war on drug traffickers . On Sunday , U.S. Defence Secretary Pete Hegseth posted a doctored image of the anthropomorphic turtle aiming a bazooka at a cargo-laden boat . Instead of his customary red cap and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ ; he hangs out of a military helicopter with a U.S. flag on his muscled arm . ( Hegseth likes his soldiers swole . ) It 's all done up to look like the classic book covers , except this one is called Franklin Targets Narco Terrorists . <p> Over the past three months , Hegseth has insisted -- with a serious boost from President Donald Trump -- that the U.S. is engaged in an armed conflict against drug cartels . Civilian smugglers have been rebranded as " unlawful combatants " or , per Franklin , " narco terrorists . " The U.S. military has attacked more than 20 boats in the Eastern Pacific Ocean and the Caribbean Sea , killing at least 80 people -- without releasing their names , providing evidence that drugs were on board , or explaining why those people posed an imminent risk to the States . The White House contends the strikes are legal , but United Nation experts say they amount to extrajudicial executions . <p> Then , last Friday , the Washington Post reported that Hegseth ordered an immediate second strike to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , who were summarily " blown apart in the water . " ( That would be a crime under both American and international law . ) Hegseth released a statement dismissing the report as " fake news , " though he did n't specify which part he disputed . He then spent the weekend posting bellicose tweets , including one that said , " We have only just begun to kill narco-terrorists , " as well as the meme that conscripted Franklin the Turtle to his cause . <p> It can be tricky , however , to pinpoint the Trump administration 's exact position on illicit drug trafficking . Mexico produces the vast majority of fentanyl , but its cartels are n't the target of U.S. military strikes . Instead , the attacks have mostly focused on boats off the coast of Venezuela , a transit hub for relatively minor quantities of Colombian cocaine . <p> Maybe that distinction does n't matter to Trump : " We 're just going to kill people that are bringing drugs into our country , okay ? " he said in October . " @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ " But yesterday , former Honduras president Juan Orlando Hern ? ndez walked out of a West Virginia prison -- 44 years short of his 45-year sentence for drug trafficking , and very much alive . Hern ? ndez had been convicted for his role in a scheme that flooded America with more than 400 tonnes of cocaine . Trump granted him a full presidential pardon on Monday . <p> A pretty serious contradiction ? Well , those seem to be going around lately . " Franklin the Turtle is a beloved Canadian icon who has inspired generations of children and stands for kindness , empathy , and inclusivity , " they wrote . <p> The Pentagon fired back with its own statement Monday night : " We doubt Franklin the Turtle wants to be inclusive of drug cartels ... or laud the kindness and empathy of narco-terrorists . " But I do n't remember that particular lesson from my childhood bookshelf . I do n't remember a story called Franklin Finds a Scapegoat , either . 
@@132037509 <p> Every few months , a new wave of critique sweeps through the trauma world , calling out guru culture , pseudoscience , and the tendency of some bestselling books to overreach . Recently , a widely circulated essay argued that The Body Keeps the Score by Bessel van der Kolk is both scientifically flawed and fundamentally harmful . Predictably , this set off a familiar cycle of tweets , TikToks , and think pieces declaring the book dangerous and the author irredeemable . <p> Whenever this happens , my inbox fills with messages from clients asking the same questions:Is the book wrong ? Am I wrong for finding it helpful ? Is van der Kolk a villain ? <p> For people whose nervous systems already lean toward self-doubt and self-blame , these debates can be deeply destabilizing . <p> There are a few truths : <p> First:The Body Keeps the Score is not evil . <p> Second : If the book helped you , you are not foolish or misguided . <p> Third : Critiques -- some valid , some exaggerated -- do not require us to abandon the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ mistakes , one being comments about Gaza , for which he later apologized , mistakes do not nullify decades of clinical contribution . <p> The real danger is not the critique itself , it 's what we do with it . Too often , people assume that if a piece of science is imperfect , the whole theory must be discarded . If a metaphor is reductive , the entire framework must be fraudulent . If an author stumbles , all of their work must be tainted . <p> This is not only overly simplistic , but all-or-nothing thinking also harms the very people we claim to protect . Rather than burn the whole house down , we can renovate the rooms that need updating and preserve the shelter that many people rely on . <h> Where the Critiques Have Merit <p> 1 . Incorrect or Exaggerated Citations <p> There is at least one fully incorrect citation in The Body Keeps the Score : a claim that traumatized children have " 50 times " the rate of asthma . The referenced study does n't discuss asthma at all , and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ This should have been caught . But research does link chronic childhoodstress to increased inflammation and higher asthma risk . The problem here is not the idea , it 's the exaggeration . <p> At times in The Body Keeps the Score , van der Kolk uses the triune brain model -- an outdated framework dividing the brain into three layers , reptile , mammal , and human . Neuroscience has long moved beyond this as it has recognized that brain systems are far more complex , integrated , and distributed . <p> And yet , metaphors like " the lizard brain " remain helpful for many people trying to understand overwhelming emotional reactions . The language is metaphorical ; it is a tool for meaning-making . A metaphor can be scientifically imperfect and clinically useful . <p> Critics argue that polyvagal theory is unproven or inaccurate . Some claims about vagal pathways remain unverified . But the broader insight -- that physiological state shapes perception , connection , and behavior -- is very well supported . Polyvagal theory works best as a metaphor for understanding safety , shutdown , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ <p> Critics also argue that van der Kolk leans too heavily on fMRI research in his book , that this research is all flawed , and therefore , his book is invalid . Early fMRI studies had methodological issues and tiny sample sizes . The infamous " dead salmon study " revealed how easily false positives could occur without rigorous statistical correction . And yet , modern fMRI research is far more sophisticated . It remains a valuable tool -- one thread in a larger tapestry that includes clinical observation , behavioral research , and physiology . The lesson here is not : " throw out all brain imaging . " It is : " interpret it as a single data point with nuance and humility . " <h> Does the Body Keep the Score ? <p> Much of the controversy rests on how people interpret this phrase . I see three common interpretations : <p> The Scary Interpretation:The body stores perfect , hidden memories that cause illness until " released . " This one is not supported by science . <p> The Metaphorical Interpretation:We live our experiences through our @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ is clinically undeniable as it maps onto the lived experience of many trauma survivors in treatment , and this one is strongly supported by science . <p> The body might not literally store repressed memories in muscles . But traumatic stress absolutely leaves biological footprints -- on inflammation , arousal , immune function , sleep , attention , and more . The metaphor speaks to something survivors know intuitively : trauma is lived in the body as much as in the mind . <h> The Ableism Argument <p> Some critics claim that because van der Kolk focuses on reducing symptoms , he implies that trauma survivors are " broken . " I struggle to see this in the text itself , though the broader cultural narrative sometimes leans in that direction . <p> Where I do agree with critics is this : When trauma is presented as the singular cause of chronic illness , two harmful things happen : <p> People feel blamed for being sick . <p> Survivors come to believe they are permanently damaged . <p> Trauma is one thread in a much larger tapestry that includes genetics @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , and pure chance . Trauma can shape health , but it is not destiny , and it is never moral failure . <h> What Can We Responsibly Say ? <p> A balanced , evidence-informed position looks like this : <p> The triune brain is a metaphor . <p> Polyvagal insights about safety and connection are clinically useful , even if the full theory is unproven . <p> fMRI studies reveal patterns , not causes . <p> Somatic therapies help with regulation , not with uncovering hidden memories . <p> Trauma affects the body through stress physiology , not mystical storage . <p> Embodiment matters , without implying blame , doom , or " brokenness . " <p> This is the middle path : scientifically grounded , compassion-forward , and clinically meaningful . <h> Why Nuance Matters <p> The least harmed people in these debates are the ones being critiqued . The most harmed are trauma survivors who finally found language that made their lives make sense . When we declare that an imperfect book must be thrown out entirely , we are not protecting the public . We 're @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ update the science , critique oversimplifications , and retire inaccurate metaphors . But we can also allow people to keep the healing they found in earlier frameworks . And our cultural conversation about trauma will mature only when we stop swinging between extremes and start renovating instead of demolishing . <p> Healing is not served by absolutism . It is served by nuance , curiosity , and the steady work of integrating new knowledge without erasing what has helped people survive .

@@132075437 <p> For almost a year , President Donald Trump has waged an unprecedented campaign against a core institution of international law , the International Criminal Court , seeking to end its work on the war in Gaza . Those governments have reiterated they have no plans to concede to Trump , and instead have made statements suggesting the court can continue functioning -- including by pursuing Israeli officials for their role in the war . <p> The ICC issued arrest warrants for Israeli Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu and former Israeli defense minister Yoav Gallant in late 2024 over alleged war crimes and crimes against humanity in Gaza , which remain in effect . @@132112300 <h> Silence is not an option <p> The Caribbean is facing a dangerous intersection of crises : the devastation left by Hurricane Melissa , the accelerating climate emergency , and now the threat of militarised conflict in our regional waters . While our people struggle to rebuild , foreign military operations are escalating , deepening the very climate conditions responsible for stronger , deadlier storms . Yet our Government remains largely silent . <p> Climate scientists confirm that Hurricane Melissa 's rapid intensification was fuelled by Caribbean waters now 1.4 ? C above average , a condition made hundreds of times more likely by human-driven climate change . Small island states like Jamaica contribute almost nothing to global emissions , but we absorb the full consequences : shattered homes , battered coastlines , and disrupted livelihoods . <p> War multiplies this danger . Research from Queen Mary University of London shows that military conflict is a major driver of greenhouse gas emissions . In the first 60 days of the Gaza conflict , Israel 's bombardment produced more than 21,000 metric tonnes of CO2 equal to burning 150,000 tonnes of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ over 400 million metric tonnes of CO2 . Today , similar climate-damaging military activities are occurring in our region . <p> For more than two months , US military assets have been active in Caribbean waters , producing significant emissions and reportedly carrying out lethal strikes at sea . Such actions raise grave legal concerns under the United Nations Convention on the Law of the Sea ( UNCLOS ) , particularly Articles 30 , 87 , 110 , 224 , 225 , and 301 , and may constitute extrajudicial killings . <h> Headlines Delivered to Your Inbox <p> Colonial patterns rooted in extraction , violence , and environmental harm continue to shape regional vulnerability , as affirmed by the UN Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change . Jamaica and CARICOM have legal tools they are failing to use . The Inter-American Treaty of Reciprocal Assistance ( Rio Treaty ) of 1947 , which allows collective defence when regional peace is threatened , remains inactive . The American Declaration of the Rights and Duties of Man , to which the US is bound , guarantees rights directly endangered by militarisation in our @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ active conflict zone . Their lives and livelihoods can not be collateral damage in geopolitical confrontations . <p> The Government of Jamaica must protect its people by rejecting militarism in Caribbean waters , activating regional legal mechanisms , and defending our right to safety and a stable climate . Jamaica must also accelerate renewable energy , halt offshore oil exploration , and adopt a decolonized climate-resilient recovery strategy , including coastal rezoning that protects ecosystems and community rights . <p> When storms strengthen and empires prepare for war at our doorstep , it is the poor who are sacrificed . The Government must act before silence becomes complicity .

@@132232194 <h> What 's behind the recent flare ups of Holocaust denial in Poland ? <p> Holocaust history prof . ( Photo courtesy of Jan Grabowski ) <p> Over the last two weeks , the Polish government has been doubling down on its official narrative that , during the Second World War , its own people were the victims of the Germans -- not responsible for collaborating in the murder of millions of Jews during the Holocaust . <p> That policy has been law since 2018 , and has led to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , including award-winning Canadian professor Jan Grabowski . Grabowski , a historian at the University of Ottawa and the child of a Warsaw ghetto Holocaust survivor , has spent years researching how ordinary Poles denounced , betrayed and helped carry out the murder of 200,000 Jews -- mostly without any prodding from the Nazis . <p> That 's why Grabowski , who has been sued by the Polish state over this issue , has been closely monitoring the recent flare-ups involving Poland , Israel , and even Germany , which began at the end of November . <p> It started on Nov. 19 , when the new U.S. ambassador to Poland -- an observant American Jew who used to run the Jerusalem Post -- told a startled Warsaw conference that it was " a grotesque falsehood " and a " historic injustice " to blame Poland for Holocaust crimes committed by others . <p> After that , a popular far-right member of the Polish parliament stood outside the gates of Auschwitz to oppose the country 's plan to adopt a new antisemitism strategy . @@132232196 <h> Munk Debate official slams anti-Israel boycott that attempted to shut down the Toronto event <p> The final vote at the Munk Debates in Toronto , Dec. 3 , 2025 . ( Credit : Ellin Bessner ) <p> The co-founder of the Munk Debates believes his recently completed event on Israel that took place Dec. 3 in Toronto proved how important it is to be able to hold respectful , civil dialogues at a time when the explosive Israel/Palestinian conflict has turned the city 's streets into flashpoints these past two years . <p> Rudyard Griffiths moderated the debate last Wednesday between four Israeli politicians at Meridian Hall . They were arguing whether it is in Israel 's interest to support a two-state solution for Israelis and Palestinians . <p> Despite a few anti-Israel hecklers inside the city-owned theatre , and an estimated 200 other protesters outside the front entrance chanting and scuffling with Toronto police , nearly 3,000 people attended the debate in person . Another 19,000 Munk members watched the livestream . <p> " So our view is that the dialogue of democracy won last night , " @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the debate . <p> " And hopefully the fact that we were able to undertake this event despite threats and intimidation levied against our organization , and against an online boycott that was organized against Meridian Hall , and TO Live , the city agency that manages the event space , that campaign of intimidation failed and hopefully a little more sanity in this city can prevail , " Griffiths said . <p> Arguing for the pro side , supporting a two-state solution , were former prime minister Ehud Olmert , and one of his former senior cabinet ministers Tzipi Livni , who was Israel 's chief negotiator in two rounds of failed peace talks with the Palestinians until 2014 . <p> The team opposing the creation of a Palestinian state now consisted of Israel 's former ambassador to Washington , Michael Oren and Ayelet Shaked , a former interior minister under Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu 's previous government who supports annexing the parts of the West Bank where the majority of Jewish settlements are located . <p> According to Griffiths , the debate topic was chosen to spark a @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ a nation , but ca n't , yet . The topic became even more timely after the Gaza war ended in a ceasefire Oct. 10 . <p> Boycott campaign <p> Several weeks before the debate , organizers began hearing through social media that some anti-Israel groups in the city were mobilizing to stop the debate from happening , or at least disrupt it . <p> Opponents decried the Munk organization 's choice to not include any Palestinian speakers . <p> A coalition of anti-Israel activists demanded that Canada bar Olmert and Livni , people they consider to be Israeli " war criminals " , from entering the country . An official complaint was filed with the RCMP and Canada 's Department of Justice , according to a news release . <p> Griffiths revealed his team took extraordinary measures to ensure their debate went ahead . <p> As is the usual practice , tickets were offered first to Munk Debate members . <p> They were not sold to the general public , but rather made available through carefully selected local Jewish community groups and other organizations more likely to welcome a @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ before they were sent a link to purchase discounted tickets . <p> While the theatre was not full , Griffiths said the debate was sold out . <p> Munk Debates co-founder Rudyard Griffiths ( centre and also shown on the jumbo screens ) launches what he called the most controversial topic in the organization 's history . ( Ellin Bessner photo ) <p> Heavy security measures outside the building included uniformed guards at the entrance doors . Ticketholders lining up to enter had to pass through airport-style security detection units . Once inside , event staff monitored the side entrances to prevent protesters from infiltrating . <p> Journalist Samira Mohyeddin was blocked from entering the debate . When she objected . claiming she had purchased two valid tickets , she was told by Munk management that her money would be refunded . Mohyeddin has been a regular attendee and supporter of the city 's anti-Israel campus encampments and street protests since Oct 7 , 2023 . <p> Although doors opened on schedule at 6 p.m. the extra security measures delayed the debate 's official start time by half an hour @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ also moderated the debate , to inform the crowd that it was Munk 's largest and " also the latest " ever debate . <p> Difficulty securing a venue <p> It was also the first time in over a decade that the debate was not held at its usual home at Roy Thomson Hall . Meridian Hall seats 3,191 and is the largest soft-seat theatre in Canada , while Roy Thomson holds 2,630 . <p> But Munk officials hinted that capacity was n't the issue . It was that they had a tough time finding a place that would host this particular topic for fear of unwanted controversy and protests . <p> " In the past with other venues that will go unmentioned , we sometimes have tense and not entirely pleasant discussions about whether our debates can in fact proceed . There was none of that with TO Live , " Griffiths acknowledged . " They were dedicated to the success of the event and I think that showed last night in the professionalism of their staff . " <p> The CJN contacted Roy Thomson Hall for a comment @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Meridian Hall is owned by the City of Toronto and managed by TO Live , which also runs the adjacent St. Lawrence Centre for the Arts and the Meridian Centre in North York . <p> Griffiths is critical of organizations who reject dialogue . <p> " A lot of cultural and arts groups have , for whatever set of circumstances , seemed to downplay people 's and groups ' free speech rights and respecting those in the context of bookings and making halls or theatres , movie theatres available , " he said . <p> Debaters unfazed by interruptions <p> As Wednesday 's Munk event began , Griffiths warned the audience to be prepared for possible interruptions . <p> Chanting from about the estimated 200 protesters outside the venue was audible inside the theatre as Ayelet Shaked led off , representing the " no " side . <p> She told the crowd why she and the vast majority of Israelis oppose the establishment of a Palestinian state . <p> " We want to live , not to be blown up on a bus or in a restaurant , not to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ or butchered in a music festival , " she said , adding that each time peace offers have been made to the Palestinian leadership over the years , the result is never peace , but always terrorist attacks and war . <p> " Insanity is doing the same thing over and over again and expecting different results , " Shaked said . <p> Shaked got through about 80 seconds of her remarks before calls of " From the River to the Sea " broke out from several points in the darkened theatre . <p> " I think you are useful idiots ... of a terror organization , " Shaked retorted . She received a round of applause . <p> Shortly after Tzipi Livni took her turn to give her opening statement , shouts of " War criminal " could be heard . <p> The audience responded with cries of " shut up " , but the heckler persisted . After a few moments , law enforcement reached the heckler and escorted them out of the hall . <p> Livni , who Munk officials describe in the printed program as " @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ " was her government 's chief peace negotiator during several rounds of failed talks with the Palestinians between 2007 and 2014 . <p> She likened Israel 's current situation to a rocky , violent marriage that needs to end . <p> " It is a realistic divorce , splitting the property for the sake of the children , our children , " Livni argued , noting that living without defined borders for the past 60 years means the country is " paying a painful price for terror and wars . " <p> " Because if we do n't separate , we are on a slippery slope toward a one-state reality between the Jordan River and the Mediterranean Sea with an endless conflict , violent , national religious conflict between our two peoples . @@132305945 <p> Never-Ending Tales : Stories from the Golden Age of Jewish LiteratureEdited by Jack Zipes ( Princeton University Press ) <p> " We tell ourselves stories in order to live " So , famously , opens Joan Didion 's 1979 book , The White Album , written while she was examining hippie-era California . This is also the core message of Jack Zipes 's new anthology , Never-Ending Tales : Stories from the Golden Age of Jewish Literature . For this is , on the most fundamental level , Zipes 's thesis : at times when simply living while Jewish was perilous , Jews told Jewish stories in order to assert their right to exist . He writes , " Storytelling and the publishing of stories became a means through which Jews could share their problems among themselves . " It 's a book about storytelling as a communal survival strategy . <p> Zipes , an emeritus professor of German and comparative literature at the University of Minnesota specializing in folklore , uses the anthology 's brief introduction to explain how the stories that follow " formed a resistance to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ with contemporary readers is to demonstrate " how Jews , whether religious or secular , found ways to survive and to find hope . " While the stories may already be known from previous translations , Zipes has newly anthologized them in order " to demonstrate how Jewish folk tales and fantasy writing can help us comprehend how deeply the Jewish Question is entwined in cultural history . " <p> This is not the first time Zipes , who is Jewish and originally from New York , has covered this type of source material . In a 1991 book called The Operated Jew and the Operated Goy : Two Tales of Anti-Semitism , Zipes had the brilliant idea to juxtapose an antisemitic 1893 short story ( Oskar Panizza 's " The Operated Jew " ) with a Jewish author 's 1922 satirical retort ( Salomo Friedlaender , " The Operated Goy " ) . The first is about a Jewish man ? -- ? effectively an anti-Jewish caricature ? -- ? who gets himself cosmetically turned into a German gentile , culminating in a wedding scene with a Christian bride @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ The second has a German gentile surgically Judaizing himself in order to snag a beautiful Jewess . ( There are countless beautiful Jewess trope stories that have been written over the years , courtesy of both Jewish and gentile authors . This is the only one , to my knowledge , in which Sigmund Freud himself makes a cameo . ) <p> " The Operated Goy , " unlike its inspiration , ends with the operation taking hold : " Now Mr. and Mrs. Moishe Kosher are living today as committed Zionists in a country villa near Jerusalem . " And the reason I know this is that Never-Ending Tales begins with a reprint of those two stories in translation . The purpose of the book is twofold : to put together a larger collection of texts in the same vein as Friedlaender 's , and to do so in 2025 . Every history book is about both the time of its focus and that of its writing , and this , though not a history book in the disciplinary sense , is no exception . <p> The anthology 's @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ question ? -- ? mainly spans the 1870s to the 1930s in Central and Eastern Europe , with occasional appearances of England and the United States . The chronology and geography thus go from the era of pogroms to the rise of Nazism . It 's possible to argue , as this book does , that this stretch constitutes the peak period of Jewish literature ( Marcel Proust was active at the time ) , but it 's an unusual interpretation . It 's certainly a bold assertion when one considers the familiar canon of postwar English-language authors like Philip Roth , Saul Bellow , Susan Sontag , and Mordecai Richler . It 's not that big-name writers are absent from the era Zipes is interested in : Sholem Aleichem and I. L. Peretz are in the mix . But this feels more like a claim about the sociopolitical importance of the stories themselves than a dispassionate assessment of their merits . <p> And that 's fine . As a matter of strict critical assessment , maybe " golden age " is a bit strong , but those years were @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ what it meant to be Jewish ? -- ? questions that would have made no sense to ask in previous eras ? -- ? emerged , were debated within the community and beyond , and swiftly took on life-or-death urgency . While these matters got debated in the political sphere , in governments and newspapers , they were also the subtext to much of Jewish fiction , which is where this anthology enters the picture . <p> Zipes defines " the Jewish question " as follows : " After the so-called emancipation of Jews in Europe and elsewhere , should Jews be accepted as full citizens and allowed to live as they wish in different nation-states throughout the world , or should they be eliminated , or confined to monitored regions ? " That 's a fine definition , but the most important thing about the Jewish question was that there was ? -- ? and still is ? -- ? a " Jewish question . " Consider who it is who would ask this , as a question . Jews do not ask " the Jewish question . " @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Jewish , but that is not what " the Jewish question " refers to . <p> To speak of a " Jewish question " is to speak of a Jewish problem . It 's a vantage point that rejects just letting Jews be . Rather than allowing Jews to adhere strongly to Judaism or to mix fully with the mainstream population depending on any individual Jew 's inclinations , askers of the Jewish question view this as a matter to be determined externally . And sometimes , historically , the answer to the Jewish question has been a simple no . <p> At its extreme , maybe even its core , the Jewish question is about Jews ' humanity . Are Jews people like others , or humanoid infiltrators ? Chances are that you , a reader of Scribe Quarterly , do n't need convincing . Chances are that you know nice Jews and not-so-nice Jews , the gamut . There 's a decent chance you 're Jewish yourself , and would find it inconceivable to doubt that Jews are human beings . But others have not always been so @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and centre ? -- ? which is to say , it does n't include a lot of information about the stories , the places and times in which they are set , and their original publication . A section at the end provides short biographies of the authors and editors whose work appears , and there is a modest array of footnotes , but the background information is limited . If the book is used in an educational context with a teacher filling in the blanks ( it 's published by an academic press ) , this is not a problem . But for a reader picking up the book outside that setting , the absence of context has the effect of gesturing at a unified Jewish past . You might be in Poland or England , in the eighteenth or twentieth century , given the range of where stories were either written or set . It 's a portrait of a civilization at a crossroads . <p> What sort of portrait do we get ? One in which , notably , the lure of the outside world always coexists @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ on interfaith romance , with central or side characters converting for the purpose of marriage . This may seem shockingly modern for that time ? -- ? or not , depending on one 's familiarity with the plot of The Fiddler on the Roof ( based on stories by Sholem Aleichem set in **29;2357;TOOLONG Russia ) , in which one of Tevye 's daughters marries out . <p> S. Ansky 's " The Pentinent " is about a Jewish man who abandons his Jewish wife and child , takes up with ( and bigamously marries ) a Christian woman , only to have end-of-life regrets about abandoning the Jewish people . ( As a Jew , I 'm perhaps meant to be moved by this , but I am wondering why he expressed no remorse at ditching his family , or rather , why the story does n't concern itself with that aspect of his trajectory . ) Along similar lines , Dovid Bergelson 's " The Convert " tells of an unpleasant Jewish woman who becomes a Christian to wed and henpeck the man she loves , who happens @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ The City Without Jews , a short satirical novel about Vienna expelling its Jews and then experiencing expeller 's remorse , includes an ongoing romance between a young Christian woman and a now-exiled Jewish artist , who poses as a Christian Frenchman to return to Vienna and be with her . <p> The unifying element in this collection of short stories is thus an intermarriage plot . There is also an eerily **31;2388;TOOLONG passage about how the gentile women of Vienna had outright preferred Jewish men for their sobriety and talents in the bedroom . You could be in a Philip Roth novel or a Woody Allen movie or a Seinfeld episode . Or more recent still : the hit Netflix series Nobody Wants This , about a " shiksa " who falls in love with a rabbi , launched in 2024 but exists in the same symbolic universe . <p> Intermarriage was always at the core of the so-called Jewish question , and also a challenge to essentialism . If Jew and gentile were meant to be these wildly different entities , what was to be made of the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ For so many reasons , intermarriage works as a lens for understanding how Jews and non-Jews understood the boundaries of Jewishness . <p> As a course reader , Never-Ending Tales would be fantastic . As a standalone book for someone interested in Jewish fiction or folklore , however , it might have benefitted from a narrower focus , either thematic ( free idea : intermarriage ! ) or in terms of the number of stories included . There is no shortage of worthy material . The two operation stories at the start are crucial , and The City Without Jews is a piece I 'd never heard of and now can not stop thinking about . It 's both intensely anti-antisemitic , defending Jews ' presence in Vienna , and at times so pro-Jewish it veers into promoting antisemitic stereotypes . Jews , in the story , are the people who keep cultural institutions and luxury shops in business . A mixed honour . Paul Schlesinger 's " The Enemies " ( ca. 1920 ) , about two Jewish soldiers , one French and one German , dying side by @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of contemporary relevance , as well as an epic punchline . <p> There were other stories that , while worthy , seemed less distinct . I ? -- ? someone who has unusually high stamina for this sort of thing ? -- ? got bogged down in the stories of rabbis and golems , which started to blend together . In the aggregate they do , however , evoke the pogrom era , and how it felt for Jews to live as prey . <p> There are a couple of moments in the book that come across as well-intended inclusivity efforts that , each for their own reasons , do n't quite work . First , the inclusion of a " ca. 1950 -- 1960 " story about a sorcerer , as " Told by a Yemenite Jew to Rachel Seri , " adds chronological , geographic , and gender diversity , with Seri one of two women presented in the book as authors of the stories . But I 'm not sure what bringing in the postwar Mizrachi experience in such a limited manner adds to a project very @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , and invites questions about what was n't included : Why not Ethiopian Jews ? Why not short fiction from 2011 ? Precisely because there 's no monolithic thing , no such thing as theJewish experience , parameters help spell out what a project is and is n't covering . Including 1870 and 1930 , England and Russia , is already quite a lot . <p> The second such concern pertains to the other female author , or maybe-author , Helena Frank , to whom Zipes attributes the book 's epigraph : " Jews will be Jews while the world lasts , and they will become , through suffering , better Jews with more Jewish hearts . " This is taken from Frank 's entry in the anthology , the 1912 story " The Clever Rabbi . " In the introduction , Zipes lists Frank among the anthology 's " less well-known writers . " But her bio in the book describes her as " a British translator who was not Jewish , " which got me curious about this fascinating-sounding woman I had never heard of . I looked @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Gutenberg ) ? an edition of Yiddish Tales , one of her books of translation mentioned in that bio . There , she is listed as the translator of an author-unknown Yiddish story , " The Clever Rabbi . " <p> Why does any of this matter ? It 's not that I object to crediting translators , or to non-Jews translating from Yiddish . Rather , it 's that Frank is presented as this obscure Jewish woman author ? -- ? bearing the same surname as perhaps the best-known female Jewish author of all time ? -- ? finally getting her due . <p> The inclusion of two pieces of non-fiction , notably Theodor Herzl 's 1896 " The Jewish Question , " an essay-length excerpt of the beginning of The Jewish State , struck me at first as an odd choice . Anyone in Jewish Studies will probably have already encountered this text . And anyone who happens to have picked up a book of anti-antisemitic fables will be wondering why a polemic is in the mix . <p> But by the time I got to it , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ choice in light of all the literal fairy tales that precede it . For if you did n't know that the state of Israel existed , if you really were reading all these materials in a context-free space-and-time vacuum , you would naturally assume that it was yet another galaxy-brained musing by a Central European Jew . Indeed , " If you will it , it is no fairy tale , " on the title page of Herzl 's classic Zionist novel , Old-New Land ( Altneuland ) , is easily the most famous Zionist literary quotation . Only the section heading word " Essays " announces that this is the realm of non-fiction . Coming on the heels of the mythic creatures and improbable coincidences of the short stories that precede it , Herzl 's depiction of a Jewish state in the land of Israel seems equally bonkers . Yet Israel exists . <p> With Herzl out of the way , the American Jewish scholar Leo W. Schwarz 's essay , " The Essence of Survival , " a **29;2421;TOOLONG musing on post-emancipation Jewish history , brings Never-Ending Tales @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Jewish modernity , but has the air of a top-notch lecture in a survey course . It functions mainly as a way of avoiding the charged historical personage of Theodor Herzl from having the last word . A book ending with Herzl might seem to be presenting Zionism as the end point of Jewish history . And since a Jewish state did not wind up demolishing antisemitism , perhaps it seemed correct to wrap up the anthology on a more ambivalent , detached note . Detached , that is , because Schwarz was a scholar , but also because he was American , a lucky thing for a Jew to be in 1935 . <p> But ending with Herzl would have been the more provocative ( in a good sense ) way to go . It was my first time reading Herzl since the events of October 7 , 2023 . First , too , of course , since the Gaza war , and Israel 's unprecedented ostracization . What struck me now was how thoroughly Herzl was writing from within his own context , that of a Europe unwilling @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ ? vet ? regarding how the local Arab population would come to regard a Jewish state in Palestine is as striking as the clear-eyed precision with which he described the predicament of his own people , which would only get more dire . ( Herzl died in 1904 . ) It 's a strange experience , reading someone at once so right and so wrong , for reasons he could not have anticipated . To read Herzl in the mid-2020s is to emerge with more questions than answers . That Herzl did not simply solve the " Jewish question " is maybe the strongest evidence that this was never a question that Jews had the power to solve . <p> As Zipes conveys in his introduction , the so-called Jewish question is still with us . But the stakes and dynamics have changed with world events : " Now the Palestinians seem to occupy the role of oppressed , while Israel appears to be the oppressor . This is one of the sad paradoxes of post-Holocaust history . What is a Jew to do when roles are reversed ? " @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ obviously not something a book about Europe from 1870 to 1930 can address head-on . What it can do , helpfully , is remind us in the here and now exactly how foreign Jews were viewed as being in Europe for much of that period . Antisemitism was about eliminating the " Semitic " or " Oriental " presence from European lands . <p> There is a subtext one sees in some anti-Zionist rhetoric that Jews had lovely lives in the diaspora , outside the odd microaggression , cheerily existing as Germans , etc. , of the Jewish faith , until a fascist madman took power . The creation of Israel , therefore , would seem like an overreaction to a historical discontinuity . The takeaway from Never-Ending Tales is not that Israel 's creation was the end-of-history capstone to Jewish modernity . Rather , it 's that Jews ' status as full Europeans was new , tenuous , and continuously under attack well before Nazism . <p> More broadly , Never-Ending Tales offers models for resisting oppressive regimes . No , Trumpism is not Nazism ; I leave it @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ classify Trumpism with respect to fascism . But it is hard not to think of the 1938 remarks of Yale Divinity School professor Halford E. Luccock : " When and if fascism comes to America it will not be labeled ' made in Germany ' ; it will not be marked with a swastika ; it will not even be called fascism ; it will be called , of course , ' Americanism . ' " <p> Whatever Trumpism is , and however unsettling it is to Canadian Jews as Canadians , it is not an anti-Jewish eliminationist movement . That does not mean contemporary resonances are absent . The City Without Jews is most notable for being a Jewish writer 's 1922 dystopian description of what would in fact soon happen : Vienna ( and not just Vienna ) expelling ( and not just expelling ) its Jews . A 2004 film , A Day Without a Mexican , has basically the same plot as The City Without Jews , but with a different minority group set in a different locale ( modern-day Los Angeles ) . In 2025 @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the events described were playing out in real life , in the form of immigration enforcement raids . <p> That said , Never-Ending Tales is also relevant for the obvious reason : antisemitism is back , if it ever went away . Antisemitic incidents regularly outpace other forms of hate crime , despite our small numbers . Hateful speech on social media , whether by humans or bots , has it in for Jews disproportionately . Anti-Jewish sentiment remains a way of revving up all manner of crowds . With that in mind , the anthology helps illustrate continuities within the history of antisemitism . Antisemites of yore may not have been reacting to the actions of the not-yet-existent Jewish state , but they absolutely saw themselves as fighting their oppressors . The Jews in these stories were not all-powerful , far from it . But antisemites saw them as a menace . <p> When I came across the concept of the Jewish question in the early 2000s , as a graduate student learning about European Jews ' emancipation , it seemed a thing of another time . Surely today @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ as The Other . That way of seeing things had to be specific to European environments where most everyone was the same thing apart from this handful of people who were another one , namely Jewish . Or , fine , maybe WASPy postwar country clubs . But in contexts where all sorts of religions and physical features abound , and overlap , how could " Jewish " not just be one category among many ? How could we still be a question , let alone of interest to anyone other than ourselves ? Zipes does not answer this , nor would I expect that of him . Never-Ending Tales offers the uninitiated , as well as the initiated , an excellent survey of where we ourselves are coming from . <h> Author <p> Phoebe is the opinion editor for The Canadian Jewish News and a contributor editor of The CJN 's Scribe Quarterly print magazine . She is also a contributor columnist for the Globe and Mail , co-host of the podcast Feminine Chaos with Kat Rosenfield , and the author of the book The Perils of " Privilege @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , will be published by the Signal imprint of Penguin Random House Canada in May 2026 . @@132310752 <h> SSP Diaries Setting the stage for global destruction ? -- Part 1 <p> IN TODAY 'S world many of us are losing sight of the fact that modern conflicts have negative impacts upon our own well-being and make it even more difficult for small island developing states to achieve developmental goals . <p> The conflicts between Russia and Ukraine negatively impact the world 's grain supply and that of other minerals needed to support science and technology ; those on the African continent hinder the development of nations in that area and their potential for global trading opportunities ; the Israeli Palestine saga has eroded the importance of the United Nations as a body of any significance in stopping or reducing hostilities ; and US foreign policy over the last 10 years has only served to be a global disservice , one that nations have grown to distrust . Sadly , many initiatives of the US have been copied by the UK and EU . <h> WORRYING TRENDS <p> These are worrying trends . In the Russia/Ukraine conflict , it would appear that the US has gained the upper @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ now reportedly have deals that give them access to the vast mineral deposits of that country that will adequately fuel their technological and military capacities . It could be argued that they now " own " Ukraine . Russia naturally might have had this goal in their offensive but can claim no such ' victory ' , and as such is forced to save face by continuing their actions until they find themselves in a more favourable position than they are currently . <p> The US gains is cause for rising tensions in Russia . This conflict is likely to continue and escalate for some time . Presently Russia is tempting and testing the resolve of certain NATO countries by deliberately conducting military operations against Ukraine over their sovereign air space , a flagrant violation of international law and the Geneva Conventions . Such actions are working in their favour because of the indecisiveness of NATO and the reluctance of that organisation to act without the consent of their largest partner , the US . This is a potential flash point that could lead to all-out war . <p> The @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ very thinly across the globe . They , through Qatar , were committed to resolving the conflict in Gaza . The actions of Egypt , Qatar and to an extent Saudi Arabia , speak to this . The strike by Israel on Qatar soil has changed all that . The Arab world has since taken steps to arm themselves against what they now believe to be a ' rogue Israeli nation ' , supported by the US . Note is taken of the fact that relationships between the Kingdom of Saud and Washington have been weakening over the last decade , especially after the assassination of the Saudi journalist Jamal Khashoggi in 2018 . He enjoyed US residency . <p> The importance of the US being able to have stable supplies of oil is what @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ behind maintaining friendly relations and in return , protection is offered by their strong military presence . The peace in the region is now threatened by a US ' rogue friend ' and there is clearly an inability to control its actions . The tensions in the Middle East are , in my estimation , far worse than reported . There is every likelihood that the Arab world will suspend their problems with Iran/Syria just to deal conclusively with Israel . This will set the stage for a further rise in tensions leading to conflicts . <h> VIOLATING SOVEREIGNTY <p> Russia also has its global designs and has an approach that is not far different from that of Israel as far as violating sovereignty is concerned . The developed Western world has unleashed a monster that beckons global disaster . The world is left to wonder , which country 's sovereignty will be violated next , by whom and on what pretext ?
